ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u2	Thank you so much for saying that. Even on my part, everyone has a nice greeting! However, I thank the gold for its good cooperation in preparing a declaration on the foreign policy of the Republic of Slovenia. It should be said that we have already discussed the first draft declaration on the foreign policy of the Republic of Slovenia on 14 November last year on the Committee on Foreign Affairs and on the Committee on European Affairs. From February to April there were three public hearings, the last public hearing was held by the Committee on Foreign Policy here in the National Assembly, before in the Council of State and also in Jabla. I am very pleased that both scientific institutions and universities, civil society, have been involved in the preparation of this declaration. Here I would specifically mention the Slovenian Society for International Law, the former ambassadors' club. So I think the debate on the declaration was extremely thorough. We have also collected comments on the website. I think it is right that after 16 years we get a new declaration, the 1999 declaration has actually been fulfilled. The main objectives were to join NATO, the European Union and the OECD. We have also achieved these goals. It is true, however, that the international environment has changed significantly at that time, including the crisis, globally, in 2009, 2010 has contributed to its changes, new forms of terrorism are emerging through the Islamic State, and Europe is facing the problem of migration. I think it is just the right time to adopt a new declaration on foreign policy. Last but not least this year is a special year – marking 70 years of the end of World War II, 100 years of the start of World War I, 70 years of the establishment of the United Nations, this year celebrating 10 years of Slovenia's membership in the European Union and NATO. And I think that we could not have caught a better moment to adopt the foreign policy declaration. I am confident that the National Assembly, with a large majority, will support this declaration. As far as the declaration is concerned, it is based on four sections. The first section discusses fundamental values, fundamental principles, including legal bases for Slovenian foreign policy; here I have to mention the Constitution, various conventions, from the United Nations Organization, the Council of Europe, not to list all. We define Slovenia as a Central European and Mediterranean country between Western Europe and the Western Balkans. The second section focuses mainly on the objectives of Slovenian foreign policy, namely security, prosperity, regulated relations with other countries, the reputation and visibility of the Republic of Slovenia, a strong European Union where Slovenia is supposed to be at its core, reinforced multilateralism. Finally, given that our nationality and even Slovenian nation is based on Slovenian language and culture, one of the goals is also the recognition and promotion of Slovenian culture in the global world. In particular, the third section deals with priorities in terms of content and geographical areas. With regard to geographical areas, let me say good neighbourly relations, cooperation with Central European countries, the Western Balkans, alpine space, the Mediterranean. Then values such as human rights, economic diplomacy, migration, climate change, intercultural dialogue, transatlantic cooperation. The latter section, however, sets out a system framework for how this declaration of foreign policy could be implemented. I'm sorry, time's up or it'd be longer. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u4	Thank you for the floor, Madam Vice-President. Dear Minister for Foreign Affairs Karl Viktor Erjavec, dear colleagues and colleagues! At its 26th meeting, on 1 July 2015, the Committee on Foreign Policy considered the Proposal for a Declaration on the Foreign Policy of the Republic of Slovenia submitted by the Government to the National Assembly for its consideration. In the course of the course of business, amendments were tabled by the coalition groups, namely, the three amendments to the preamble, the two to Article 1, the three to Article 2, the two to Article 3 and the two to Article 4. The members and members of the Committee also received proposals for amendments to the Committee at Articles 1, 2, 3 and 4 of the proposal for a declaration at the table. In its consideration, the Committee took into account the opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service of 2. 6. 2015, which examined the proposal for a declaration in terms of its consistency with the Constitution, the legal system and the legislative and technical aspects and the text of the proposal for a declaration, commented that the declaration expressed general views on foreign policy issues and was therefore a political and non-legal act, which, in view of the practice of drafting and adopting texts of declarations in the National Assembly, required adequate numbering. The Head of the Legislative and Legal Service added that the proposals for amendments to the Committee followed the opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service. Members and members of the Committee on Foreign Policy were also informed of the opinion of the Committee of the State Council on International Relations and European Affairs of 23. 6. 2015, which supported the proposal for a declaration. A further explanation of the proposal for a declaration was made by the proposer, Minister for Foreign Affairs Karl Viktor Erjavec, who stressed that a large substantive debate had already been held in the framework of various public presentations of opinions and the inclusion of various political views and views of the professional public. He went on to express his affection for the amendments of the coalition parliamentary groups, pointing out in particular the amendment to the first indent of Article 4 of the declaration, which defines the National Assembly as the institution of the foreign policy of the Republic of Slovenia, and the amendment to Article 4, paragraph 3, of the declaration, which, in the opinion of the Minister, would be significant, since the annual reporting to the National Assembly on the development of international relations and the situation of the Republic of Slovenia in the international community would be extremely important in terms of the notification of the implementation of the guidelines of the Slovenian foreign policy. Members and members of the Committee on Foreign Policy actively participated in the debate, and some of the emphasis on the declaration was praised until certain were critical. One of the opposition parties' concerns regarding the role of the Republic of Slovenia within the European Union and the NATO Union was also expressed. On the basis of paragraphs 131 and 171 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, the Committee then adopted the amendments I had previously mentioned. In accordance with Rule 128 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, the Committee voted on the entire proposal for a declaration and adopted it. In the light of the amendments adopted, the Committee, pursuant to Article 133(1) of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, prepared the text of the supplementary proposal for a declaration incorporating the amendments adopted. The updated proposal for a declaration is also an integral part of this report, which you have received in writing. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u5	Thank you very much. The following is a presentation of the views of the groups of Members. The word is given by the Alliance of Allies of Alenka Bratušek, for which Mr. Peter Wilfan has the floor.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u6	Dear Vice President, Minister, colleagues and colleagues! The National Assembly is considering a new proposal for a declaration on foreign policy to replace the 1999 declaration, which focused mainly on Slovenia's EU and NATO membership. Since Slovenia has achieved all the strategic objectives set out in that declaration, and also because the situation in the international community has changed significantly since 1999, it is high time for Slovenia to set its new strategic objectives and plan the work of foreign policy for the next few years. Therefore, the question is whether the country needs an external policy strategy at all, just rhetorical. Slovenia needs a new strategic document to determine its role in the world. It is not acceptable to leave the search for these answers to the authorities on a case-by-case basis. Moreover, every time an external policy problem arises, it becomes a pressing question why Slovenia is without a strategic document. Our foreign policy must also be pragmatic, but only within the framework laid down by the fundamental priority areas and the tasks set out in the declaration. In the Alliance, we support the way in which the text of the declaration was drawn up by the Minister for Foreign Affairs. Since the declaration will represent a kind of constitution for Slovenian diplomacy, it is right that the process of design and acceptance took a little longer, as the purpose of the declaration is also to achieve the unification of Slovenian policy. We wish to congratulate both the Minister and all those who have participated in the preparation of this declaration, for we are confident that he will provide good support to Slovenian foreign policy, for the successful implementation of the design process and the broad professional and political consensus reached. Nevertheless, we note that we need a declaration that will not be limited to a time period and which will not be a static document, a living text that will adapt to the current world situation or allow for dynamic adaptation of foreign policy not only through changes in the strategy but also through changes in the declaration itself. The Council is becoming more complex and the role of Slovenia and the European Union is changing. The Declaration sets out Slovenia's widest possible presence abroad, in accordance with economic interests, and a consular activity that supports international economic cooperation. We agree that the scope of Slovenian diplomacy for the country is extremely important. Since the early 1990s, the main objective has been to become a member of the European Union, the OECD, and we have therefore put most diplomatic and consular representations within the European Union or on the European continent. Now, in a time of economic crisis, it is more reasonable to look in the international space for a node which could be interesting for the Slovenian economy or where the Slovenian economy also shows interest. In the absence of this interest, intensifying efforts for economic diplomacy does not make sense. As regards one of the more important issues identified by the declaration, this is cooperation with the European Union, and we would like to draw attention to the risky expression of "implication of the interests of the Republic of Slovenia as active members in the core of the European Union and the placement of the Republic of Slovenia in the core of the deepened and enlarged European Union". We believe that the Republic of Slovenia should stand for a strong or deepened European Union. The fundamental strategic interest of Slovenia is the preservation of a strong and deepened EU and its institutions. Slovenia is one of the economically weaker in the EU and is unfortunately politically peripheral, but Slovenia should not agree to the logic of the EU at two speeds and different trains, such as French-German or other concepts. In our view, we should work towards the enlargement and deepening and effectiveness of the European Union. It seems to us that this would be the right path for Slovenia to follow. It's over. We can read in the declaration that we are committed to a state-oriented foreign policy, the continuity of principles and values, the sovereign equality of countries, the self-determination of nations and the high standards of human rights. These are long-term strategic orientations, which should not only be the content of the new declaration, but rather the reality of our foreign policy. Small countries that do not have economic and military power in today's international space can act sovereignly only with the so-called value-based policies, which are subject to respect for fundamental human rights and freedoms in the world, committed to peace and non-violent resolution of the issues at stake and the equal participation of all nations and countries in the world. Only through such foreign policy will we build the reputation of a country known for its values and principles in international relations. We hope that, with the recognition of Palestine, we will also demonstrate our commitment to such a state-oriented foreign policy. In the Alliance, we will support the declaration and we will also support the proposed amendments.

The involvement in NATO is therefore important. ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u16	Thank you, Madam Vice President. Good-bye, everybody! We have a long-term framework for Slovenian foreign policy ahead of us. In the United Left of Slovenia in this context we simply do not see. This framework, which, under the pretext of Euro-Atlantic integration, advocates everything that the European Union and the Union represent, is not the framework for ensuring equal relations between nations, solidarity and the general emancipation we represent. This framework is a framework that has produced, still produces and will produce social and cultural inequalities, creates greater inequalities between the centre and the periphery, between rich and poor countries and increases differences between rich and poor people within countries themselves. That is why we must distance ourselves from the three passwords which are the essence of this document and offer an alternative. These three slogans are "Slovenia in the core of a strong European Union", "Then as a fundamental framework for national and European security" and "strengthening transatlantic cooperation". The first password, the core of a strong Europe, clearly speaks of two Europes or a Europe of two speeds. The free market of the European Union and economic policy adapted to the large countries in the centre have led to a stalemate in the weaker countries of the periphery. The symptoms are the unemployment of a quarter of the population and more than half of the young. The blind deepening of the economic and monetary union announced by the declaration will further exacerbate the situation. The alternative is a different Europe that adapts policies to the needs of the Member States. This alternative is not only principled but also pragmatic. Slovenia is not in the core of the European Union. The experience of the southern European countries and our own experience of taking over the euro show that we will not reach the core market in a free market situation. For a password about a strong Europe that will be able to exercise the interest of members as one of the key global players, another interest is hidden – the continuation of imperialist geopoliticalism. Our interest is a different Europe, one that will stop interfering with other continents and its neighbourhood. This alternative is not only desirable, but also realistic, and its fulfilment does not mean anything more than approaching the idea of a large European family that is actually solidarity, equal and fair. That's not it now. Such a European Union, as we now know, has produced a series of humanitarian crises, has been involved in all major military actions by the United States, Afghanistan and Iraq, for example, and has participated in the destabilisation of Libya and all of North Africa. He doesn't do anything in the case of Palestine. The actions of such a European Union are produced by the collapse of the social state, poverty and general misery. The actions of such a European Union sustain humanitarian crises. Such a European Union is still a fortress of Europe. Second password. Then there is no basis for national and European security. National security is based on friendly relations with neighbouring countries and their own defence forces. It is clear that NATO is not the foundation of European security, but the main factor of destabilisation from Ukraine through the Middle East to the South Pacific. The main and real alternative to this non-essential relationship is only the non-blocking position of Slovenia based on neutral countries such as Austria, Sweden or Finland. The third password of enhanced transatlantic cooperation covers the continuation of the European Union's subordination, harmful to American foreign policy. Then there's no longer a defensive link, if that ever was. Its only function is to preserve American hegemony in the 21st century. The common European foreign policy must be independent of the US, or it is better that it is not at all. The aggressive American policy against the Russian Federation and China is leading to a confrontation that can only be prevented by the European Union's neutral stance. Finally, the belief that European-American privileges in international relations are justified and inevitable is contrary to the democratic will of most of our citizens. A survey by the famous Gallup Institute, for example, showed that 52% of Slovenians do not approve of the functioning of the US administration. After the disapproval rate, we're even in front of Russia. This is just one indicator that shows that there is still a very vivid anti-imperialist attitude in our country, which has been adorning the struggle for the northern border, the Yugoslav volunteers between the Balkan wars and the first world war, the fighters of the national liberation struggle, and also the policy of the undeclared. Therefore, according to our assessment, the declarations are not only contrary to the principles of the United Left, but also to the majority of the population. Of course, we know from experience that citizens generally oppose neoliberal policies such as privatisation, and this does not remove the government. If we want an equal, social, solidarity and just Europe, we must also demand it and try to build it. The proposed declaration simply does not express this. Under the pretext of some new direction of Slovenian foreign policy, this declaration only deepens the policy, which means the nemo nodding of dictatorships from Brussels and Washington. In 1999, we wrote in the previous declaration, quote: "In the field of global and regional economic relations, Slovenia advocates solutions that ensure full employment, higher quality of life and conditions for economic and social progress and development." We have not reached these principles. With the current declaration, which he doesn't even mention, we certainly won't. Because of all this, we cannot and will not support this declaration in the United Left. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u17	The New Slovenia Group – Christian Democrats, Mr. Joseph Horvat, has the floor.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u18	Thank you again, Madam Vice President. Dear Mr Foreign Minister, distinguished colleagues and colleagues! In the Encyclopædia Britannica, foreign policy is defined as, I quote, "general objectives that guide the activities and relations of one country in their interaction with other countries. The development of foreign policy depends on weighing matters within each country, policies or behaviour of other countries or plans to make progress on specific geopolitical objectives. Leopold von Ranke stressed the primacy of geography and external threats in the design of foreign policy and later authors point out domestic factors. "Diplomatic diplomacy is a tool of foreign policy. Wars, alliances and international trade are, in fact, all manifestations of this." End of quote. And now to Slovenia. First, let us take a closer look at the role of the National Assembly in foreign policy matters in Slovenia. In Slovenia, the basis for cooperation between the Government and the National Assembly in the field of foreign policy-making lays down the Law on Foreign Affairs. This Article, addressed to the Carrier of Implementation and Cooperation, provides that "in the framework of the powers of the Government, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs shall carry out external affairs in accordance with the general guidelines laid down by the National Assembly". In particular, the Article states that "The Ministry of Foreign Affairs shall prepare to the Government, the National Assembly and the President of the Republic, as well as to other State bodies the professional basis for adopting positions, assessments and actions in the field of foreign policy where matters relevant for the implementation of the foreign policy of the Republic of Slovenia are concerned. In cooperation with other competent authorities and services, he shall take care of the Slovenian community in neighbouring countries and Slovenia around the world and shall carry out other tasks laid down by this law and other regulations." Colleagues and colleagues, in the December 1999–July 2015 timeline, a lot has happened, in Europe, globally and, of course, in Slovenia. And in this context, at the October meeting of the Committee on Foreign Policy of the Minister in the context of the questions and initiatives sent, I asked or gave an initiative on how the working material of the declaration on the external policy of the Republic of Slovenia is. The Minister responded correctly and specifically as he then brought working material or draft declaration on the external policy of the Republic of Slovenia to the Committee on Foreign Policy in October 2014. The Committee considered that this working draft declaration, as a political document, was the appropriate basis for further discussion, and at the same time the Government undertook to see a draft or proposal for a strategic document prepared and adopted by the Government before the final decision on the declaration. ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u42	Honourable President of the National Assembly, honourable Members and Members of Parliament! Allow me to say a few words at the beginning of the second reading of the supplementary proposal of the Fiscal Rule Act. I know you're all very well aware that this is an extremely important subject, with several aspects. Undoubtedly, the most important substantive aspect is that the law lays down a framework for managing public finances in a sustainable manner. Respect for the rule will halt the trend of public debt growth due to excessive current public spending. It is, therefore, that, as a result of us, within the constitutional framework that we have set ourselves, we are setting up a strict legal framework within which we will ensure adequate public financial efficiency and accountability. From a formal point of view, of course, it is very important that the law can only be adopted by at least a two-thirds majority. This means that the adoption of this law requires a high political consensus, which, of course, goes beyond just a coalition. So let me say right away that I am very pleased that you have all worked together in great efforts to reach this consensus. The law is also important from an international point of view as Slovenia is exposed at European Union level as the only Member State that has not yet regulated the fiscal rule in more detail in its legal order. This, in the face of the uncertain situation resulting from the complications with Greece, makes Slovenia's position on international financial markets worse, which, of course, also affects the cost of borrowing in the economy. So now is the time for us to adopt the fiscal rule law. As you know, it should have been adopted within six months of the amendment of the Constitution in May 2013, more specifically Article 148 of the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia. The draft law sets out the way and timeframe for the implementation of the principle of medium-term balance of income and expenditure of the State sector referred to in Article 148 of the Constitution. The law also provides for a corrective mechanism if a derogation is found from this principle. However, since the derogation is not always due to economic policy decisions, but also to exceptional circumstances, the proposal also provides for a way of taking action in such situations. The law also establishes the Fiscal Council as an independent state authority to monitor the implementation of the fiscal rule. Finally, the proposal also provides for a transitional period for the gradual structural balance of public finances. Details will also be presented by our Minister of Finance, Dr Dusan Maramor, in the discussion below. Let me say that the complementary bill you will be discussing today contains substantively meaningful proposals. Such as have been presented to us in interviews in recent months and all the nomotechnic corrections needed to make the text clearer. At the same time, the law remains complete, which is extremely important, as it is likely that due to the two-thirds majority, which is the condition for its adoption and also, of course, the change, it will probably remain unchanged for a long time. Let me stress that, in cooperation with you, with political parties, Members, both those of the coalition and those of the opposition, have had a lot of coordination, except, of course, with the United Left, which has already given up immediately after the first meetings to participate in this coordination. Both the coalition and the opposition party, as well as the representatives of both national communities, the Italian and Hungarian nationalities, have made constructive contributions to this discussion. Of course, she also participated in this discussion all the time, both legal and economic. Therefore, I believe that the law is now ready for adoption in the second reading as well as for admission in the third, i.e. for final adoption and enforcement. By accepting or by all of us together, you will demonstrate your serious intention to manage the state finances well and responsibly. It is not only a constitutionally binding act, but also a very important act of state. I wish you a lot of success in today's debate, a lot of constructive debate, and I hope that we will finally achieve what is important for Slovenia to us to adopt the Fiscal Rule Act. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u44	Dear President, all representatives of the government, colleague and colleague! The Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy is held at its 11th regular session. 6. 2015 As a parent working body, the proposal for a Fiscal Rule Act submitted by the Government to the National Assembly for consideration and adoption was considered by the Government. The Committee also received an opinion from the Legal and Legislative Service, which examined the law submitted in terms of compliance with the Constitution and the legal system and from a legislative and technical point of view, the Service made some general comments and also to individual articles of the bill. In addition, the Committee also received the opinion of the Commission of the State Council for the Economy, Crafts and Finance, which supports the proposal of the law. In accordance with Rule 129 of the Rules of Procedure of the State Council, amendments to the draft law have been tabled by the Joint Left, the Slovenian Democratic Party and the coalition parties of the Modern Cent Party, Desus and SD. Meetings of the Committee were attended by the President of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia, representatives and representatives of the Ministry of Finance and the Legal and Legislative Service. The opening presentation was presented by the President of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia, Dr. Miro Cerar, who said that the law was important not only because of the constitutional commitment, but mainly because it lays the foundation for sustainable public finances. He pointed out that a broad political consensus, reflected by the required two-thirds majority, was needed for adoption. He warned that it was important for the law to remain consistent and complete even as it would be difficult to change in the future. He also mentioned the fact that Slovenia, within the euro area Member States, is the only one that has not yet regulated the fiscal rule in its legal order in accordance with the commitments made. He briefly presented the process of drafting the law and a number of substantive coordinations with representatives of parliamentary parties, representatives of national communities and with the Legislative and Legal Service. Economic and financial experts and a special expert group participated in the drafting of the law, which also participated in the drafting of Article 148 of the Constitution. He also thanked all of them on these occasions. In the introductory supplementary explanation, the Minister of Finance explained that with the adopted law Slovenia is very late, also with regard to the implementation of the directive, as Slovenia is still looking forward to a sentence. The Minister further presented in detail the key elements of the law and the proposed substantive changes in the light of the first version, with particular reference to both inequalities and to the composition and organisation of the fiscal council. In the debate, some members and members of the Committee expressed their concerns and opposition to the proposal for a law because they felt that it would not bring about a balance of public finances. In addition to monetary autonomy, Slovenia was considered to lose the fiscal leverage it could use in the event of a re-crisis. They also considered that the law provided for the excessive role of the fiscal world, which would act independently. They drew attention to the possible consequences of the social bill, the excessive cuts in public spending already in 2015 and its impact on development policy. They are also interested in whether calculations have been made on the impact of the law on economic growth. They further considered that it would be necessary to amend the Rules of Procedure and also the Law on Public Finance. Some have warned that Slovenia needs the adoption of the law to allow some flexibility in the recession and predictability of the conjuncture, which referred to the so-called Swiss formula. They also wanted clarification on the formula used in the bill. However, some members expressed their support for the bill in the debate and felt that it was right to correct the unconstitutional situation and that the law was adopted by a two-thirds majority, so that not every future government would be subject to change. It is right to ensure the long-term sustainability of public finances for future generations, while maintaining certain flexibility in the law in case of extraordinary events, with particular attention to the potential impact on the most vulnerable groups in society. They also considered it important that the Fiscal Council is an independent and professional body. At the end of the discussion, the Minister answered the questions raised and provided further clarifications. In accordance with the second paragraph of Article 130 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, the Committee has considered the amendments tabled to the article of the draft law. The Committee adopted amendments to the coalition parliamentary groups and rejected amendments to the SDS and the United Left. In accordance with Rule 128 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, the Committee voted on and adopted the articles of the proposal. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u46	Thank you very much. Dear Members! Allow me to explain in a little more detail here, in addition to the basic concept of how this law was drafted, what is the difference between the law which was submitted to the General Assembly in December last year and the importance of the law itself. As the Prime Minister has already pointed out, by adopting the implementing law on fiscal rules, we are already very late. Since it was envisaged that the key provisions of the directive would be transposed into the Fiscal Rule Act, but due to delays in the adoption of the law just prior to the action by the European Commission, certain procedures are already under way, proceedings before the EU court, the EU court may determine a fine which, taking into account the weight and duration of the infringement, could amount to around 500 days. We are also late to transpose the provisions of the fiscal pact, which we committed ourselves in 2012 to ratifying the international agreement on stability, coordination and governance in the Economic and Monetary Union. In this case, too, due to the failure to comply with the Treaty, the EU court could impose a fine of up to 0.1% of gross domestic product, which is more than €37 million. However, with already limited public funds, monetary penalties are the last thing we would like right now. With regard to the drafting of the law alone, I would like to say the following. In drafting the law, we continued the work of the former government. That is why we have taken as a basis the draft law, which was about a year ago, but to a large extent not fully aligned with the parliamentary groups. We have upgraded this framework in the direction of flexibility permitted, which is still permitted by European fiscal policy rules. It is the granting of a structural deficit of 0,5 % of gross domestic product at the time of the recession, the exclusion of one-off expenditure from the ceiling of expenditure calculated on the basis of inequalities, and the open definition of exceptional circumstances in accordance with the Stability and Growth Pact. Flexibility with these elements is crucial for a country without its own monetary policy, such as Slovenia, where economic shocks can mainly be addressed through fiscal policy. However, it should be stressed that flexibility is not intended to dilute a rule that will allow unchanged public financial policies to continue. The rule of the balanced budget is a strict limitation that will require much more adjustment in order to phase out the structural deficit currently running around 2.5% of gross domestic product. This rule will allow us to ensure that Slovenia does not create an additional public debt during normal economic times through the economic cycle for current public spending. That's the point. The bill contains the following elements. The central part is the provisions which operationalise the principle of medium-term balance of the State's budgets without debt, as provided for in Article 148 of the Constitution. The medium-term balance shall be assessed by reference to the value of the structural balance of the State sector as a whole, which shall not be negative in the medium term on average and shall not exceed 0,5 % of the gross domestic product in a given year. This rule should only be waived in times of emergency, which are defined in accordance with European public financial rules as a serious economic downturn or an emergency which the State has no influence on, with significant consequences for public finances. The scope of the derogations in accordance with the proposal is confirmed by law by the National Assembly. The bill also provides for a corrective mechanism where derogations from the fiscal rule are found. In this case, the Government shall draw up a proposal for a programme of measures to eliminate the derogation from the rule. A transitional period is also foreseen for the gradual correction of the structural deficit of the State sector, which currently amounts to around 2.5% of GDP. This has to be reduced in accordance with the dynamics envisaged in the Stability and Growth Pact during the transitional period, which, in the first phase, means the elimination of the excessive deficit we are planning this year and, in the second phase, the reduction of the structural deficit of around 0.5 % of gross domestic product per year. In line with this dynamic, which is also included in this year's stability programme, structural balance of budgets is expected to be achieved in 2020. This transitional period is very important as it will allow for the gradual adjustment of public expenditure on public financial capacity and the state. In doing so, achieving as much economic growth as possible is crucial and spending growth must be limited. In order to comply with the rule during the transitional period, the growth of public spending must be lower than economic growth, but the rules also allow for a moderate increase in expenditure at a time of sound economic growth. A large part of the law is also intended to regulate the so-called Fiscal Council as an independent state authority to prepare assessments of the compliance of public financial policy with fiscal rules. The draft law thus provides for the composition and method of appointing members of the Fiscal Council, its tasks and other elements necessary for its establishment. All of these are the basic elements of the draft law, as was transmitted by the Government to the National Assembly in December. However, in line with the commitments made in the context of the first reading of the bill, on the basis of the comments made in many interviews with parliamentary groups and various experts, a complementary text of the bill was also prepared. Key substantive changes relate to the inclusion of inequalities in order to determine the ceiling for expenditure in Article 3 and to certain organisational aspects of the Fiscal Council. The way in which the principle of medium-term balance is implemented without debt was written in the basic version of the bill only in the word. Due to the demands of the opposition parliamentary groups, Article 3 was supplemented by two inequalities to determine the ceiling on expenditure. The first of the inequalities provides for the maximum authorisation of expenditure in a given year, in the period when the level of GDP is below the potential, this is at the time of the recession. The imbalance provides for some flexibility in the management of public financial policy as it allows for a structural deficit of 0.5 % of GDP. The other of the inequalities, however, sets the ceiling for expenditure per year for a time when the level of GDP is above the potential, i.e. at the time of economic expansion. Taking account of the other inequality ensures that public financial surpluses will be created in good economic times, and they will depend on the structural deficits created in bad economic times. The imbalances determine only the ceilings and the planned level of expenditure of the State sector in a given year will also have to take into account the past and envisaged future fiscal policy in order to ensure a structural smoothing of public finances on average throughout the economic cycle. However, another major substantive change concerns the appointment of members of the Fiscal Council and the restrictions on employment in that body. The members of the Fiscal Council are to be proposed in accordance with the amended bill only by the Government, which means that three different applicants are no longer envisaged than in the basic government proposal; the President of the Republic, the Bank of Slovenia and the Government were then envisaged. In order to elect the members of the Fiscal Council, a two-thirds majority of votes will be required to ensure their impartiality in the National Assembly. Such a solution will help to ensure that only experts enjoying a broad reputation are appointed in the Fiscal Council. Some organisational aspects of the Fiscal Council have also been changed as the employment limit for the President of the Fiscal Council has been raised from at least 20 to at least 50 % of full-time work and for members of the Fiscal Council from a maximum of 20 to a maximum of 50. The amended bill also limits the number of public servants who can be employed as professional staff in the Fiscal Council to four persons. This restriction seeks to prevent the establishment of the Fiscal Council from increasing the number of public servants by ten. Since, on the other hand, the Fiscal Council will be able to conclude cooperation arrangements with other institutional units of the State sector to prepare analyses and provide data for the Fiscal Council, with restrictions on the employment of the Fiscal Council, we have not made it impossible to carry out its tasks or to limit its independence. This is also illustrated by the fact that the Fiscal Council will have its own budget, with which it will have a free budget. In summary, we have taken into account the substantive proposals that have been presented to us in a number of interviews in recent months, while making sure that the law remains complete. At the end of this presentation, I would like to say something else about the effects of the fiscal rule on public financial policy. The adoption of the law does not in any way imply any automatic modification or reduction of social rights. However, it must also be borne in mind that, after all years of crisis, Slovenia's GDP is now 7% lower than before the crisis. Public spending needs to be adjusted. The capabilities are now smaller, so the rights cannot be restored to a pre-crisis level today despite economic growth. If we take into account another 3% of GDP for interest, it is almost 10% less, 9%. With all the necessary systemic changes and adjustments, we will continue to talk. Any structural change requires a change in sectoral legislation. By no means will these changes be the result of some unnecessary commitments made at international level, but this is necessary because of ourselves and future generations. And that is why today's decision to confirm the Fiscal Rule Act is whether we will afford even more today than we have and deny ourselves and our offspring a secure future and prosperity, or whether we will also take care of the long-term social security of our citizens and the promising future for young people in Slovenia. So much for the intro. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u50	Thank you for the floor, Mr President, Mr Prime Minister, Minister of Finance and other representatives of the government. First of all, Mr Cerar said that with this fiscal constitutional law that we are adopting today, we are applying an obligation that only Slovenia has not yet fulfilled throughout the European Union. Three things have to be said here. In fact, for the first time, the international treaty that we have acceded to introducing a fiscal rule into our legal order was not mandatory, and, for example, the Czech Republic and Great Britain did not sign it. Another time. This international treaty, even after we have signed it, does not require us to introduce a fiscal rule at the constitutional level, as Slovenia does, but simply requires us to put a fiscal rule in the legal order. So we could accept it by a simple majority of 45 votes, and there would be no problem with changing the constitution if it turned out that the fiscal rule would have negative effects, which in the United Left we are very afraid will be, and in this case it would be significantly easier to correct it, because now we will need 60 votes in Parliament or an absolute two-thirds majority for any corrections to be adopted today. And the third time. The Prime Minister said that we had already withdrawn from the negotiations in the United Left. Here we have essentially expressed these two concerns to the government, namely, for the first time – why at constitutional level, why not simply delete the fiscal rule from the constitution, and then the government can accept it as an ordinary law. Secondly, we wondered why we have a stricter regulation than the international treaty. In fact, not only does the fiscal rule not have to be introduced into the constitution, but the second difference with the international treaty and our order is that the international treaty requires that the state may have a maximum of 0,5 % of the structural deficit on the medium term, as the Minister has also pointed out in his opening speech. In Slovenia, however, we have introduced a stricter provision into the Constitution, which says that it must have 0%, so also this 0.5% manoeuvre space that could be left on the middle hand, we have actually taken ourselves. Thirdly, we have not received an answer to how this formula prepared by the government will work in good times. And that was, somehow, also the reason that we decided that we could not support fiscal rules in this form in any way. Now, perhaps if I explain a little bit more about what the problem with the fiscal rule is. First, if we look at economic history, especially post-war, we will find out the following things. Firstly, no developed capitalist state applied a fiscal rule after the war. Secondly, the fiscal rule was not applied by any European country without its own currency before the crisis. Thirdly, the fiscal rule is in some ways now a constitutional commitment to saving, which, in fact, both the IMF and the rest of the European Union admits that it has proved to be highly counterproductive in the crisis. If I could afford a little walk through this history, I'd like to highlight the following things. Perhaps a slightly more distant example of the effects of fiscal consolidation on crisis resolution is found in the 1980s. Until the vote, there is still time to vote, so that perhaps the Members who have decided to vote for this law today, I recommend that you visit the parliamentary library and borrow a doctoral dissertation of Mojmir Dark, titled World South in the trap of debt, where it shows in what way the concept of the crisis that we introduce into Slovenia and Europe by introducing a fiscal rule has sunk the entire world in the 1980s, so that the whole world still remembers it today under the name of 'lost decade', and we repeat this mistake 30 years later in the European Union. Secondly, it is not necessary to go so far as to see the effects of fiscal consolidation during the crisis, in fact we can look at a purely domestic case. If we look at the European Union and the US and their responses, fiscal and monetary, to the crisis, we will find out two things. Firstly, in the United States, significantly less orthodox efforts have been made to resolve the crisis, namely, by means of so-called quantitative release, by significantly more relaxed fiscal policy, while the European Union has opted for fiscal consolidation, savings and the European Central Bank not to buy state bonds. If we look at the economic results, the economic balance of these two economies, the EU and the United States over the last seven years, we will find that the United States had recovered from the crisis a year after its outbreak and that today or 2014 it had 108% of GDP against the pre-crisis level, it is said, 8% more than they had before the crisis. In the EU, however, we have not had just one recession, but because of the fiscal consolidation in 2012, we have slipped into another recession, from which we have only just now gathered, and the result is that in 2014 we have 99% of GDP in the European Union according to the level before the crisis, it is said that the difference is 8% of GDP between one and the other way of resolving the crisis. And today, in fact, this way, which has proved to be completely flawed in both social and economic aspects, we introduce or enforce as a constitutional law. Even if we look at a fully domestic case, Slovenia, we will see that we have already recorded economic growth in 2010 and 2011, and then, because of fiscal consolidation, we are very violent in those years, in 2012 in particular, we have slipped back to the second recession, from which we were raised only in 2014. In some ways, Minister Marble acknowledges these harmful effects. In fact, in the hearing we had a few months ago, when SDS proposed a fiscal rule with the so-called Swiss formula, Minister Marble has repeatedly made very clear in his speeches of position, which are very close to what I have now said, I will just say a few quotations. Firstly, the Minister agreed at the time that saving has a markedly negative impact on economic growth and employment, as he said, quote: "The European Stability Pact is already extremely restrictive and is therefore the subject of many discussions, and here is the big question in Europe whether it is even appropriate, especially given the difference in growth between the United States, which has rejected this as inappropriate, but have its monetary policy, where economic growth is now around 4% of gross domestic product, and in the EU, where economic growth is around 0.8%." And then it continues, and as a key reason to oppose the then proposal of the SDS fiscal rule, it sets a limit on the flexibility of fiscal policy compared to international obligations. He says, "You have all the research you want. This would reduce economic growth and employment and have a negative impact on everything that depends on it, and as you know, the whole country depends on it." Thus, in his previous reading, Minister Marble agreed that the fiscal rule has negative economic and social effects. We can read in the Ministry of Finance that the experience from Slovenia in 2012 also shows that large-scale fiscal consolidation has a strong negative impact on economic activity. From this I conclude that we agree that we must not impose fiscal rules in this way, because it will harm us not only socially but also economically for a long time. Then the next issue, which is problematic, is that when we talk about the Swiss formula proposed by the SDS, such a formula can be implemented in Switzerland, which is a country with its own currency and Slovenia does not have its own currency, and in the currency area where monetary policies are mainly taken for the benefit of the centre, is said to be Germany and the northern countries, but Slovenia, we can imagine it, is somehow attached to the former German brand, and even though the less competitive economy than Germany, we have virtually 8 years since the adoption of the euro we have not devalued our currencies. Therefore, now that we have monetaryly tied hands, accepting this fiscal coercive jacket is deadly for such a country. This is also what Minister Maramor said, which, if I quote him again, said: "And the European Stability Pact, which I have said is very restrictive, is much more appropriate for Germany than for Slovenia, which is exposed to much higher shocks than a small very open economy. The ECB's monetary policy is essentially focused on the economic situation in Germany and other large euro area members rather than on Slovenia. However, Germany has no stricter rules than European rules." This is a quote from Minister Marble six months ago, and today we are adopting stricter rules than those in force in the European Community and in Germany. Once again, there is no need for a constitutional level, and secondly, the structural deficit could have a minus 0.5% in the medium term following the signing of the contract, not 0% as we have committed ourselves. The Minister will probably reply that these were his comments on the Swiss formula, but I think we are quite speaking against the fiscal rule in general, and it should also be stressed that there are no differences between the government and the Swiss formula that the Minister may be trying to present. In fact, they both require 0% of the structural deficit on the medium term, by saying that the Swiss formula is a little more brutal in bad times, for example, if a fiscal rule was adopted when the opposition SDS proposed it, they should be cut into public finances already in 2015 for 700 million euros, which means, you can imagine how many cuts to education, health, social and so on, but the government formula is problematic because it is a bit nicer in bad times, which is why it is so much more problematic in good times. In fact, if we take into our hands a simulation of the fiscal rule drawn up by the Ministry of Finance and look at, say, the period 2000-2004, we see the following trends: Slovenia had a constant budget deficit of around EUR 500-600 million, had an economic growth of between 3% and 4%, and its debt fell below 30% in 2004 despite constant deficits, due to moderate economic growth and inflation. It is said that the country can have long-term or medium-term unbalanced public finances, but if it has some moderate economic growth and smart public spending policy and moderate inflation, it can either regulate the budget or it can regulate debt in this way. As I said before, Germany, for example, after the war in the last 70 years, had never had a flat budget, with the exception of 2014, and therefore its debt from the London Memorandum in 1953 was never a problem. But let me get back to my original point. If we take these years from 2000 to 2004 and look at how the government formula would work in 2002. This year, Slovenia had 3.8% growth, 600 million deficits, but since it was a good year, it should have 50 million surpluses after the Swiss formula and 100 million surpluses after the government formula. Thus, for 700 million in 2002, when Slovenia's GDP was 23 billion, public spending should be reduced by 700 million. If I were to illustrate what that would mean. In the same year, expenditure on all monetary social benefits amounted to EUR 346 million. So if all the social benefits were to be abolished, we'd only be halfway through the fiscal effort. Then there would be a need for layoffs in the public sector, pensions cuts, health care, education and so on. We're right in front of Finland and Italy. And not only in front of all Eastern European countries, but also in front of all Southern European countries. The reason for this is that we had a traditionally strong public sector that held this society up and allowed it to develop fairly equally and in solidarity for all. ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u59	Mrs Urska Ban has the floor.
ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u60	Mr President, thank you so much for your words. Honourable Prime Minister of the Government, Honourable Council of Ministers, Honorable Fellows and Fellow Members! Today, we are in the final stage of parliamentary consideration of the fiscal rule law, which has been in place since January 2015, when we Members have voted 79 votes in favour and I think 5 against it has been considered appropriate for further consideration. To date, this bill has experienced some both substantive and nomotechnic additions or corrections that we have postponed in the form of coalition amendments and also adopted at the last regular session of the Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy. As has already been mentioned several times today, by means of a proposal or by adopting this law we are both late under the amended Constitution, Article 148 of the Constitution in 2013, the transposition of directives relating to budgetary frameworks and the transposition of the provisions of the fiscal pact. In our parliamentary group, we believe that we have been very constructive in this parliamentary process for the past six months, since we have been dealing with this law, both the coalition's parliamentary groups, the opposition, the two national communities. The text of the law that we have on the tables today, which we are going to decide on, has improved in particular to our common benefit, the benefit of our public finances and the future of our country. It is only a coincidence that we are adopting this law just in times of considerable financial and other circumstances, here I am also thinking in the light of the events that are taking place in Greece, which we have already heard about today, have listened to and which, of course, also affect Slovenia and the whole of Europe. And just so that it doesn't happen to us or that we don't go the way we can follow it today, or that we don't end up like the old saying: First the country takes the credit, then the credit takes the credit, and eventually they take the country. That's why I think it's important that this law is being passed today. I will conclude here with a debate on Greece and other topics and will focus on the very content of the proposed law and amendments tabled by the SDS and the United Left. The version of the law already drawn up by the previous government, as has already been said today, has been upgraded mainly in the direction of allowing adjustment, allowing flexibility permitted by the European rules on fiscal policies, in particular in the direction of promoting economic activity in poor and cooling overheating economic activity in good times, taking into account Article 148 of the Constitution, which requires the medium-term balance of public finances. This is mainly the case in three points: firstly, that a structural deficit of 0,5 % of GDP during the recession, secondly, is the exclusion of one-off expenditure from the ceiling of expenditure calculated on the basis of the proposed inequalities in the law and, thirdly, the definition of exceptional circumstances which are consistent with that of the Stability and Growth Pact. We believe that such an adjustment is necessary for a country such as Slovenia, which is a small open economy and does not have its own monetary policy, as shocks in economic activities can mainly be addressed through fiscal policy. However, despite these adjustments, which I have mentioned in the proposal, although some of us have heard, feel otherwise, that there is a strict limitation written down, which will require a few more adjustments in order to phase out the structural deficit that is now moving around 2.5% of GDP. This rule, which is an essential emphasis, will enable us to avoid creating an additional public debt in Slovenia during normal economic times through the economic cycle for ongoing public spending. The proposed law, as has already been said today, has been supplemented by two inequalities in Article 3 through the findings of the parliamentary debate since January. Unequalities to determine the ceiling for expenditure, where one determines the maximum allowed expenditure within a period when the GDP level is below the potential, are said to allow a structural deficit of 0.5 % of GDP at the time of recession, while the other relates to good time and sets the ceiling for expenditure per year at the time of economic expansion. Taking this inequality into account ensures that public financial surpluses will be created in good economic times. Whether in good times it is necessary to pursue a public financial policy that will not only compensate but also create structural surpluses, is largely dependent on the structural deficits created in bad times. The rule in this form allows accumulated structural deficits from bad times to be replaced by structural surpluses in times beyond potential GDP growth. In accordance with this proposal, the structural balance may only be negative in a given year and, on average, in accordance with the provisions of the Constitution, it must be at least offset in the medium term or in excess, in accordance with the amended Article of the Constitution. At this point, our understanding of the fiscal rule is different, as previously presented to us by our colleague Shircelj or as proposed by SDS, that the so-called Swiss formula is more appropriate to calculate the ceiling of expenditure instead of the so-called inequalities. However, compared to what is proposed in this law, with these two inequalities, in bad times it does not allow further promotion of economic activity through the application of fiscal policy to the maximum permissible structural deficit of 0.5% of GDP. It requires continuous structurally balanced public finances both in good times and in bad times. The Swiss formula also limits the way to determine the state of the economy in the cycle to the calculation of the trendy level of GDP, and this government bill refers to the European Union methodology. And this rule, proposed by the government, ensures that public debt will not increase due to current public spending. At the same time, structural balance over the medium term ensures that debt will decrease as a percentage of GDP. This is also a key indicator of the sustainability of public finances from the point of view of international financial markets. However, debt may be increased due to exceptional events or because of unique effects. We have already had such cases, including the case of Greece's rescue. On the other hand, it will decrease through the effects of privatisation. All of this is not included in the framework set out in this rule proposed by the Government. Maybe a few more words about the Fiscal world. The fiscal world is much more specifically defined in the final text of the law that we have on the table today than it has been in previous versions of the law. Four articles of the 18 proposed law refer specifically to the Fiscal Council, which allows this Fiscal Council to be established without the adoption of additional legal bases or additional laws. It is true that in the basic government version of the proposal, three drafters, the President of the Republic, the Government and the Bank of Slovenia, were thought to have ensured impartial membership in the Fiscal Council, but through the parliamentary procedure from the first to the present reading of the proposal, such an arrangement has been assessed as too complex. Therefore, it has also been amended on the basis of talks and arrangements. It was also considered the solution resulting from the procedures for the appointment of governors and vice-governors and members of the Court of Auditors, where the President of the Republic has the primary role, but this arrangement was not assessed as the best in terms of the constitutional profession. In line with the complementary proposal, the members of the Fiscal Council are proposed by the Government, all three members are proposed by the Government and the State Assembly by a two-thirds majority vote. In doing so, we wanted to ensure that there will be indeed experts in the Fiscal Council who enjoy a broad reputation in our society. In addition, the government will not be able to name what was also one of the exposed eyesets of favored experts in the Fiscal Council. In most countries, the Fiscal Council has no direct impact on public finances in the form of automatic consequences that would trigger the opinion of the Fiscal Council. It is true that there is no universal model of operation and organisation of the Fiscal Council that would be suitable for all countries. The rules of the European Union set out the tasks of the Fiscal Council, the rest being governed by each country individually. We must take care of ourselves or place this body within our institutional framework, clearly define powers and responsibilities, ensure client or political independence, provide resources for the functioning of this body and for communicating it with the public. At the level of this implementing Fiscal Rule Act, we believe that we have met these criteria set by EU legislation. The independence of that body is ensured, as foreseen in the previous draft law and has already been aligned with the parliamentary groups. As an independent body, however, it is also envisaged not least in the coalition contract. As a new budgetary user, financial resources will be provided for its operations and all three members of this Fiscal Council will need the support of Members and Members, i.e. Parliament, with a two-thirds majority. Where I see the key power of the fiscal world. I think it stems from its impact on public opinion, through the possibilities of communicating with the public and the public with its assessments. At the same time, the responsibility of the Government and the National Assembly in the design and management of fiscal policy is maintained. More important and crucial, in my view, is who will be the members of the so-formed Fiscal Council and how they will successfully monitor budgetary policy, from planning to implementation. And only with its functioning will the Fiscal Council create the necessary credibility of this institution and respect. The role of the Fiscal Council is to monitor compliance with the plans and to produce related assessments. As these evaluations will be public, the Fiscal Council will have an extremely important impact on the formulation of public opinion. The Government will be obliged to draw up its position to the opinion of the Fiscal Council and to make it available to the National Assembly by explaining which assessments it will take into account and which it will not. The fact that the Fiscal Council, as has already been said, does not directly shape fiscal policy is not a matter for Slovenian refugees, such as Slovenian origin, but also for other EU Member States. His role, here I think that our fiscal council is not overly loose, as was said or exposed in the previous debate, but his role comes from the EU rules, where he clearly writes, quote: "Although fiscal authorities are primarily responsible for ensuring compliance with correctional mechanisms, national supervisory institutions would be a key element in enhancing credibility and transparency. The principle to be taken into account or based on which the opinion of these supervisory institutions would either be taken into account or should be justified by the Member States concerned on why they did not take it into account, would ensure that such an assessment would not simply be ignored without prejudice to the responsibility of the fiscal authorities with regard to policy-making." Here I see the very right and proper regulation of the Fiscal Council, including in this bill. The law also provides for a transitional period for the gradual correction of the structural deficit of the State sector. This means that in the coming years the structural deficit of the sector will need to be gradually reduced by some 0.5 percentage points per year. This phase will prevent economic growth from requiring excessive cuts to the extent of public financial expenditure in order to comply with the fiscal rule. Under the assumption of economic growth, this is by no means an infringement of existing social rights, but certain appetites will have to be tackled and public expenditure will have to grow at lower rates than economic growth. However, once we have achieved structural balance between revenue and expenditure, we will be able to create reserves in good economic times, real budgetary surpluses, and in the poor, as I said before, also budgetary deficits. In order to comply with this rule, it will be crucial to achieve the highest possible level of economic growth on the one hand and to limit expenditure at a rate lower than GDP growth. The key idea is that due to economic growth, our public expenditure will not explode. Slovenia's GDP is still 7 percentage points lower in 2014 than it was in 2008. Well, if I can just get a little more of this together for the rest of my life. Obviously, the constitutional placement of this principle and the adoption of this law have ensured that things will not change due to political opportunism, for example by amending some of the ZIPRS novels we have done so far. It is a matter of responsibility, and this has been highlighted today, by our generations to their offspring and the cessation of the burden of debt on the next generation. Respect for the Constitution and this law, if we adopt it today, will therefore require every single Slovenian government to adjust spending accordingly, the expenditure side of the budget is true. In our parliamentary group, we want Members today to adopt this bill with broad support, including those parliamentary groups that have not supported this law in the parliamentary procedure so far. In particular, because we will commit ourselves to further responsible behaviour and to future public finances, which are certainly the basic condition for the further development of our country. The fiscal rule will put the executive power ahead of the obligation to propose and the legislative authority will oblige it to adopt budgetary laws that are balanced each year. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u108	Thank you very much. Let me just answer some of the allegations. First of all, with regard to these inequalities, that they are not clear, uniform and so forth, they are ambiguous. In fact, these inequalities determine what the maximum amount of expenditure is. That's what they say. There can't be more than that. And that's unequivocal, clear and so forth. There can be less expenditure, then, of course, the deficit is lower or the surplus is higher. But when you have the same or the smaller one, it's said, expenses can be one or the smaller one, nothing else. And that's just a record of what's in the Stability and Growth Pact and what's in the fiscal pact. Where is the key difference now with the Swiss formula? The key difference is that this allows even more than 0.5% of the structural deficit at a time when it goes poorly. But that means that you have to have more than enough in times of good times. So there must be an inequality, which means that when things go well, you can't have that much expenditure, you can only have less. And so there must be an inequality that you can bring in what you lost at a time when you had a bigger deficit. Why do you have to have a higher deficit when you have a bad condition? We have two economic theories, one Kenyan one, one neoclassical one. And I say, no one works at all times and in all economic conditions. One works better in bad times, it's Kenyan, and one works better in good times, it's neoclassical. They both have elements when they can work in both. And then when things go bad, then you need to help the economy with additional demand, that's the Kenyan model, and that's what I've been explaining since the first reading of this law, that it's essential to help. You can help in two ways, or with a more expansive monetary policy, which is what the European Central Bank is currently doing, when this quantitative release helps the European Union economy to essentially drive what the US Central Bank has done a long time ago and the American economic growth has grown very much. Or fiscal policy or both. America did this to both of us. So she went to the deficit, helped with the additional demand of the state and also spent extra money. We do not have a monetary policy in Slovenia, so we cannot help with the extra issue of money. The policy of the European Central Bank, as I explained at that time, is more focused on the whole euro area, where it has the greatest weight of the countries that are the largest. Not looking at what Slovenia would help, but watching what helps the entire area, and the whole area is dominated by Germany, Spain, France and so on. This does not help us when we need it, and this has been the case in this crisis. So what's left for you? There's no other option than an additional fiscal incentive. And here this one is very limited. 0.5% of gross domestic product is a very small fiscal stimulus. Now in this crisis there were 3% or 4% of gross domestic product, we have 0.5, because these are the rules of this Stability and Growth Pact and we cannot go through it. But if we don't take advantage of it, then we do have a problem. Then we have nothing in our hands when there is one shock or this small 0.5%. That is why we have proposed essentially this change that this record of the Stability and Growth Pact and the Fiscal Pact has come in the form that is now enshrined in this Article 3 of our Act. That's why we have a problem with the Swiss formula, because then it's all automatic and you have nothing to answer with. And this can be very bad for the economy, because you have to help it, as it is with us right now, you have to help it to come into being and start working hard again. Then you have to take that away, and when the economy goes well, you have to save it. There's nothing here. And you have to save more than a Swiss formula. That's all logic. So please understand, this is no two-pronged solution, this is the basic logic adopted by the European Union. The European Union was aware that it could not write the Swiss formula because it has so different situations across different countries within the euro area and even more within the EU area. That is why this is essentially a record of what is now possible in EU legislation and we have taken advantage of these options here. As we have it written down, it has Germany written down, exactly the same. Germany is in a much better position than we are, because the European Central Bank is also responding to the needs of Germany, not to the needs of Slovenia. And it's also 0.5, says it can take advantage of this deficit when it's absolutely necessary. That's it, thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2015-07-10-SDZ7-Redna-10.ana.u114	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, Members! The bill before you is prepared for two reasons, first by removing administrative obstacles and deregulation of one of the professions and further by aligning with the changes in European legislation as well as domestic ones. The proposal for a law removes the conditions for carrying out representation operations before the customs authorities. We call them forwards in a popular way, since 1 May 2016 it will no longer be necessary to carry out specific tests of knowledge. That is when the new EU Customs Code, which directly regulates representation before the customs authorities for authorised economic operators and special regulation in national legislation, is no longer necessary. However, a person who gains the status of an authorised economic operator will be able to carry out business without any restrictions in all EU Member States in accordance with the new customs code. The proposed amendment regulates the possibility of selling non-community goods at airports and ports. In this regard, provisions concerning the control of the sale of goods in frontier shops as well as the relevant criminal provisions are also being amended. The provisions of Articles 33 and 35 of the Law on the implementation of customs rules which form the basis for the issue and define the content of the rules on the implementation of the Single Administrative Charter, the electronic operation of the customs administration and other forms used in customs procedures are further amended. In this part too, it is necessary to comply with the amendments to European legislation. The next change is also part of this set. These are procedural provisions relating to the implementation of the European Regulation governing the action of customs authorities against goods suspected of violating certain intellectual property rights. These are smuggled goods or counterfeits. Since a new regulation has been adopted at EU level in 2013 and is new to this area and repealing the regulation in force, we are removing the provisions in question from the law because we will regulate them within the national regulation for the implementation of the European Regulation, such as in other countries or in other cases such regulations. We align the draft law editorially with the provisions of the Financial Administration Act and define in more detail the term 'customs authority', which we no longer have defined in other regulations by repealing the Customs Service Act. Dear Members and Members! In the light of all the above, I propose that the proposal for a law amending and supplementing the Law implementing the customs rules of the European Community be supported in both the second and third readings.
